Micheel J. Montesano, 730 7th St. Buffalo, N.Y.

Very well regardad by the Italian people in Buffalo and aspires

Italian affairs and tekes pride in the fact that he is on the
City War Council by appointment of Meyor Joseph Kelly. Believe
he is very well informed as to who is who emong the Italians
and would be a good source of information with respect to anything
concerning Italians in Buffalo.

Cours.

SECRET Nios

The war will not last forever. One day the fighting
will stop and the gigantic task of the reconstruction of
Europe will have to start. The United States have clearly
indicated a desire to participate in the rehabilitation of
the European continent. Italy is one of the countries that
the American people will certainly try to help in their
spiritual economic and financial recontruction. Not only
what but both countries - the United States and Italy - are
longing to restore to its fullness their historic traditional
friendship erasing as quickly as possible the tragic interlude
of the war. That day may not be very far. It is time to
start getting ready for it. There are things that happen
only too suddenly. Being ready now and having at least some
clear outlines of what could and should be done along the
lines of the rvstoration of the old ties of friendship bet-
ween It.iy and the United States is essential if mistakes and
delays are to be avoided at a time when mistakes and delays
should be avoided.

The period of readjustment through which Italy is bound
to go for a number of years especially in the field of its

SECn.

shattered economy is going to be very hard and difficult.
The business of liquidating a war,even a victorious one, is always
a difficult and complicated one. Infinetely mora difficult
and complicated will be the business of closing a chapter ag
painful and tragic as the present war has peen for Italy.
America wants to help in this gigantic task. It is to the
interest of the United States to assist the Italian people
during the period of its readjustment. It is to the interest
of the Italian people to regain the American friendship. The
vision of the future yet to come opens immense pemapectives.
Yat @e very few people know exactly what can be done and how it
is going to be done.

There is - as yet - no comprehensive balance sheet of
the relations between the two countries. Lots of people
talk about them but nobody knows what they really were
before the war started and hobody has until now explored all
the potentialities of an italo american cooperation in the nost-
war scheme of things.

Because - among other thinge ~ Italy herself is not very
well known. The outer world knows Italy either as one of the

great meccas of the tourist trade, the land of the magnificent

arechedogy, or as the scene of some greatly perplexing and
not always understandable recent history. Not many realize
that behind the ruins and the headline names there are in
Italy many other thirgs,some of them good, some of them bad,
some of them indifferent, but all of them worth while knowing
if one has to deal at all with the Italian people.

A more complete knowledge of Italy ought to be, therefore,
at the basis of any long range policy affecting the Italian
people.

Such a knowledge would clarify and simplify quite a
number of problems that seem and have seemed all along in-
credibly paf'ling to all those who had to deal with modern Italy.

It is especially and vitally important to start exploring
the possibilities of an italo-american post-war cooperation now,
right in the midst of the war, because a study of this kind might
vastly help to indicate to the Italian people a way out of its
aditficulties and create an interest in the allied victery showing |
that the United States is anxious to help Italy not only to

survive but also to regain, with dignity, her place among the

free nations of the world.

Ii, is absolutely essential to indicate to the Italian
people the existence of an alternative to the policy followed

far so long by the government of Rome and the desirability of
a change from the standpoint of the national interest of Italy.

The inevitable task of a gigantic and complex redori-
entation of the whole italian life may be made much easier
through the spreading among all strata of {talians of the feeling
that America, their old traditional friend is still with them.

The creation of a center of italian studies would greatly
help to complete the knewledge ani the understanding of Italy
in America providing at the same time an organ that could become
in due time a channel for the transmission cf american ideas
and ideais to Italy.

This center,although closely in touch with the U. S.
Government should be privately run and if possible privately
financed,

The firat objectives that the center should have ought to
be a series of strictly factual aurveys of the italian economic
and social conditions in order to have ready all the data
necessary to prepare the return of Italy to a system of free

econony.

The very fact that an effert of this kind should be
attempted in the United States in the midst of the war would
help tremendously to dispel the fears of the Italian middle
clazses and the Italian working people that a rapid change
would plunge them into chaos, uncontrollable inflation,
wiping out of all savings and endless suffering.

The center should start gathering the vast material of in-
formation on Italy available now in the United States, and
elaborating it in public monographs or private memoranda for
official use, as soon as possible so that it could be of some
help to all those who are interested in Italy and the inevitable
resumption of relations first and friendship afterwards between

the two countries.

the question addressed to uss

hat the French are proposing to preadcast to Italy in Iteléan gives
rise to misgivings. In our opinicn, their radio work will doubtless do more
har then good. Caniit reasonably be expected that Italiens, independently of
Fascists, wili lend 4 willing car to a defeated people, woo in times of peace
were indifferent or contenpiuous towards Itely? There are many influential
Italians, by no means Fascist, eho would react unfavorably to Freneh broadcasts
and would probably find in the French broadcasts reasons for giving their moral
support to the Fasclats.

Anti-British feeling in Italy is a definite obstacle to overcome. Anti-
French feeling is widespread in Ttely; why, then, arouse it further? Obstacles
to a United Nations plen for psychological warfare shoulé be reduced to a mini-=
mun, not increased.

It would probably be to the pest interest of our county and of the
United Nations if the French could be persuaded to undertake no broadcasts at
all to Italy, or if the French propaganda could be ticoordipated" with Allied
propagande in such & manner that it would be given little opportunity to become

You will, remember you spoke #8, me a few deys ago
about an investigation that ONT had mede, im which it hud

of war to nembers of their families.

It would appear that this report is not correct, and
tr, explanation I am attaching a memorandum which I have just
received from ROGER HOLLINGSHEAD, who handles Itelian affairs
for us in New York.

Lists of Italien prisoners, with the names of their
fabilies,or friends in the United Statea, which are published
in the Mazzini Society newspaper, "Nasioni Unite," are supplied
4q\ the Mazzini Society confidertially by ourselves. We re~-
aive these reports secretly from our British friends, who
have requested that we make them available to MESSHS, TARCHIANI
and CIANCA. We have sent copies of. these lists to the FBI,
which has told us thet they have no objection to their com-
munication to the Mazzini people for publication.

I do not believe that it vould be advisable to
inform anyone as to tke origin of these lists or of the way
‘fn, which they ere hawiled.

Like him -- are wuy-stations on elaborate “srapevines" which do, regularly and
eonstantly, get the news from London. (American stations are almost entirely
"blocked out” by "jamming". )

As another winter has started with more suffering than was expected --
as the end seems farther and farther away -- the Italians react first as ordi-
rary buman beings accustomed to hardship but passionately eager for the simple
comforts of normal life, hating pain, treasuring life as a good, an end in it-
self. Thon they react as patriotic Italians who fear for their homeland.
later, much later, they react as Fascists, or anti-Fascists, as Democrats.
Finally as friends of freedom, justice, morality, which to most of them are
remote, abstrac” formulae.

Intensely intelligent, Italiens react favorably to Anglo-Saxon broad-
casts because they recognize their foundation in objective fact, in truth.

But their reactions are essentially passive. Broadcasts from London deliver
body blows to their conception of the war as truly national and to their con-
sequent patriotic willingness to "see it through" at any cost. Still they
dread what the Garmans might do to them and to ltaly too much to dare to react
openly, materially. So proadcasts directed to Italy are ineffective if they
merely emphasize Italian sufferings, point out absence of suffering in the
Anglo-Saxon world. To have any effect, such broadcasts must be specifically
and unumistakeably American; they must be transmitted from such places and in
guch manner as to make them really audible. In material, they must contain
trustworthy information which will make the Italians recognize Anglo-Saxon
power as actually, materially, more fearsome than the German variety! And,

in thia contexture, power is specifically milivary.
